People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. Hi, Doctor. Thank you so much for seeing me, I really appreciate you taking the time. 

Um, well, I hope I'm in the right place, but I guess what brings me in is... well, it's a little embarrassing to admit, but I keep getting up when I'm supposed to just stay put. It's nothing terrible, I just like to be on the move, you know? But it's happening a lot during client interviews—I'm a private investigator—and during my tutoring sessions, and even just sitting at my book club. 

I always try to play it off, like I'm just getting a glass of water or stretching my legs, but... people are definitely starting to notice that I can't just sit and listen. I really don't want to come across as rude or unprofessional to anyone. Does that make sense? Is that the sort of thing you can help with?

Oh, um... yes, I suppose I do. I mean, I really try to keep it out of sight so I don't bother anyone! But... well, like when I'm stuck at the computer, you know, combing through records for a case? But it's happening a lot during client interviews—I'm a private investigator—and during my tutoring sessions, and even just sitting at my book club. \n\nI always try to play it off, like I'm just getting a glass of water or stretching my legs, but... people are definitely starting to notice that I can't just sit and listen. I really don't want to come across as rude or unprofessional to anyone. Does that make sense? Is that the sort of thing you can help with?\n\nDoctor: When you’re in situations where you’re expected to stay seated, do you also find yourself fidgeting or feeling physically restless?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... yes, I suppose I do. I mean, I really try to keep it out of sight so I don't bother anyone! But... well, like when I'm stuck at the computer, you know, combing through records for a case? I guess my foot is usually bouncing under the desk. Or I'll catch myself clicking a pen over and over. \n\nIt's just a little excess energy, I think! But, to be completely honest... sometimes I'm shifting around in my chair so much that I actually lose my place in what I'm reading. Which is a bit silly for someone my age, I know, and I really try to control it. I hope that doesn't sound too terrible? Is that the sort of thing you meant?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. But it's happening a lot during client interviews—I'm a private investigator—and during my tutoring sessions, and even just sitting at my book club. \n\nI always try to play it off, like I'm just getting a glass of water or stretching my legs, but... people are definitely starting to notice that I can't just sit and listen. I really don't want to come across as rude or unprofessional to anyone. Does that make sense? Is that the sort of thing you can help with?\n\nDoctor: When you’re in situations where you’re expected to stay seated, do you also find yourself fidgeting or feeling physically restless?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... yes, I suppose I do. I mean, I really try to keep it out of sight so I don't bother anyone! But... well, like when I'm stuck at the computer, you know, combing through records for a case? I guess my foot is usually bouncing under the desk. Or I'll catch myself clicking a pen over and over. \n\nIt's just a little excess energy, I think! But, to be completely honest... sometimes I'm shifting around in my chair so much that I actually lose my place in what I'm reading. Which is a bit silly for someone my age, I know, and I really try to control it. I hope that doesn't sound too terrible? Is that the sort of thing you meant?\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt people or blurt out answers before they’ve finished speaking?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness... well, I really try not to be rude! I think it's more that I just get a little... over-eager to help? \n\nLike, for example, if I'm at the office and I overhear the junior detectives discussing a lead, or... or even just a neighbor chatting out in the hallway. I tend to sort of... jump right in. I'll start asking questions or offering advice before they've even asked for my opinion. \n\nI always mean well, I promise! But then afterward, I'll suddenly realize that I completely barged into someone else's conversation. I feel so terrible when it hits me that I've intruded like that. I just get so caught up in wanting to be helpful, you know? I really hope that doesn't make me sound too nosy or overbearing... am I explaining that okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble sustaining your attention when you’re listening to someone or working on a task?\n\nPatient: Oh, actually... no, I don't think I struggle with that. I really pride myself on being a very good listener! \n\nI mean, with my line of work, I really have to pay close attention to the details when a client is speaking, or when I'm working through a long file. I can follow along and stay focused on the task just fine, I promise! It's really just... well, like I mentioned earlier, my body just wants to be moving *while* I'm listening or working. But my mind stays right on track. \n\nI haven't noticed any issues with my actual focus at all. I hope that's okay? Does that make sense for what you're looking for?\n\nDoctor: Were these restlessness and impulsive-interrupting behaviors already present before you were 12 years old?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... yes, I suppose they were. I mean, I really don't want to sound like I was a problem child! I was always very bright and eager to learn, I promise. \n\nBut, well... if I'm being completely honest, my grade-school report cards did usually have a little note from the teacher saying I couldn't seem to stay in my seat or wait my turn to speak. Just a little too enthusiastic, I think! My mother still teases me about it, actually. She always jokes that I treated every single classroom like a crime scene that I just *had* to pace back and forth through to figure out. \n\nIt was never meant to be disruptive, though, I swear! I just had a lot of energy, even back then. I hope that doesn't make me sound like I was a terrible student? Well... I mean, communication is just so important in my line of work, you know? I always want to make sure my clients feel fully informed. But... I suppose, sometimes, I might get a little carried away. Just out of enthusiasm, really!

Even when... well, even when the other person is giving me clear signs they're done or ready to move on. 

I really don't mean to dominate the conversation, I promise! I just get so focused on making sure they have all the details and that I'm being helpful. I hope that doesn't make me sound overbearing? It's really just because I care so much about doing a good job for them. Is it normal to get a little chatty like that when you're passionate about your work?

Well... I do like to think of myself as a very energetic person, you know? In my job, you kind of have to be ready to move. But... oh, I suppose if I'm being completely honest... this is actually sort of what brings me in to see you today.

I was always very bright and eager to learn, I promise. \n\nBut, well... if I'm being completely honest, my grade-school report cards did usually have a little note from the teacher saying I couldn't seem to stay in my seat or wait my turn to speak. Just a little too enthusiastic, I think! My mother still teases me about it, actually. She always jokes that I treated every single classroom like a crime scene that I just *had* to pace back and forth through to figure out. \n\nIt was never meant to be disruptive, though, I swear! I just had a lot of energy, even back then. I hope that doesn't make me sound like I was a terrible student? Is that the kind of history you needed to know?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever lose track of what people are saying because your mind drifts off?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I wouldn't say that at all! Like I said, I'm really quite good at listening. I think it's so important to give people your full attention, especially when they're coming to me for help. \n\nI mean, sure, sometimes if a meeting goes on for hours and hours, I might feel the urge to get up and stretch my legs, but my mind is still right there with them. I don't just... drift away or ignore people. I really try to be as attentive and respectful as possible, I promise! I hope I'm not giving the impression that I don't care what people are saying, because I really, really do. Does that sound like a normal response?\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes when you’re doing work or other tasks that require attention to detail?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. I really pride myself on my accuracy. In my line of work, you simply can't afford to make careless mistakes, you know? A missed detail in a background check or a surveillance log could ruin an entire case, so I'm always incredibly thorough. \n\nI mean, I'm sure I've made a typo here or there—everyone does, right?—but I always double-check my reports before I submit anything. I don't rush through things or just gloss over the important parts, I promise. I'm very diligent about my work. I hope that's what you were hoping to hear? I just wouldn't want you to think I'm sloppy or unprofessional.\n\nDoctor: Do you often lose things you need for tasks, like keys, paperwork, or your phone?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I wouldn't say I'm someone who loses things. I actually try to be very organized! As a private investigator, keeping track of evidence and paperwork is just... well, it's essential. I have a whole system for my files and my keys and everything. \n\nI mean, I suppose there are times when I might set my phone down and forget which room I left it in, but that's just because I'm moving around so much, right? I always find it right away. I'm definitely not the type of person who is constantly losing important items or showing up unprepared. I really try to keep everything in its proper place. I hope that makes sense? I wouldn't want you to think I'm disorganized or irresponsible.\n\nDoctor: Do you often talk more than other people, or find yourself talking a lot without meaning to?\n\nPatient: Well... I mean, communication is just so important in my line of work, you know? I always want to make sure my clients feel fully informed. But... I suppose, sometimes, I might get a little carried away. Just out of enthusiasm, really! \n\nLike, I'll catch myself talking through every single angle of a case or a client meeting, just trying to be perfectly thorough, and before I know it, I've basically filled all the space in the conversation. Even when... well, even when the other person is giving me clear signs they're done or ready to move on. \n\nI really don't mean to dominate the conversation, I promise! I just get so focused on making sure they have all the details and that I'm being helpful. I hope that doesn't make me sound overbearing? It's really just because I care so much about doing a good job for them. Is it normal to get a little chatty like that when you're passionate about your work?\n\nDoctor: Do you often leave your seat in situations where you’re expected to stay seated?\n\nPatient: Well... Like during my client interviews, or my tutoring sessions, and even at my book club. I'll just pop out of my seat. I try to make it look natural, like I'm just getting a glass of water for someone or checking the window, but... well, people are definitely starting to notice that I can't just sit and listen. \n\nI really don't mean to be disrespectful or disruptive, I promise! I just get this overwhelming urge to be on my feet, and before I even think about it, I'm standing. I hope you don't think I'm just being rude to people? Oh, well... as far as paying attention goes, I was actually a very good student! My grade-school report cards always made a point to say that I was bright, which was always nice to read. I always wanted to do a good job for my teachers.

Thank you so much for seeing me, I really appreciate you taking the time. \n\nUm, well, I hope I'm in the right place, but I guess what brings me in is... well, it's a little embarrassing to admit, but I keep getting up when I'm supposed to just stay put. It's nothing terrible, I just like to be on the move, you know? But it's happening a lot during client interviews—I'm a private investigator—and during my tutoring sessions, and even just sitting at my book club. \n\nI always try to play it off, like I'm just getting a glass of water or stretching my legs, but... people are definitely starting to notice that I can't just sit and listen. I really don't want to come across as rude or unprofessional to anyone. Does that make sense? Is that the sort of thing you can help with?\n\nDoctor: When you’re in situations where you’re expected to stay seated, do you also find yourself fidgeting or feeling physically restless?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... yes, I suppose I do. I mean, I really try to keep it out of sight so I don't bother anyone! But... well, like when I'm stuck at the computer, you know, combing through records for a case? I guess my foot is usually bouncing under the desk. Or I'll catch myself clicking a pen over and over. \n\nIt's just a little excess energy, I think! But, to be completely honest... sometimes I'm shifting around in my chair so much that I actually lose my place in what I'm reading. Which is a bit silly for someone my age, I know, and I really try to control it. I hope that doesn't sound too terrible? Is that the sort of thing you meant?\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt people or blurt out answers before they’ve finished speaking?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness... well, I really try not to be rude! I think it's more that I just get a little... over-eager to help? \n\nLike, for example, if I'm at the office and I overhear the junior detectives discussing a lead, or... or even just a neighbor chatting out in the hallway. I tend to sort of... jump right in. I'll start asking questions or offering advice before they've even asked for my opinion. \n\nI always mean well, I promise! But then afterward, I'll suddenly realize that I completely barged into someone else's conversation. I feel so terrible when it hits me that I've intruded like that. I just get so caught up in wanting to be helpful, you know? I really hope that doesn't make me sound too nosy or overbearing... am I explaining that okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble sustaining your attention when you’re listening to someone or working on a task?\n\nPatient: Oh, actually... no, I don't think I struggle with that. I really pride myself on being a very good listener! \n\nI mean, with my line of work, I really have to pay close attention to the details when a client is speaking, or when I'm working through a long file. I can follow along and stay focused on the task just fine, I promise! It's really just... well, like I mentioned earlier, my body just wants to be moving *while* I'm listening or working. But my mind stays right on track. \n\nI haven't noticed any issues with my actual focus at all. I hope that's okay? Does that make sense for what you're looking for?\n\nDoctor: Were these restlessness and impulsive-interrupting behaviors already present before you were 12 years old?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... yes, I suppose they were. I mean, I really don't want to sound like I was a problem child! I was always very bright and eager to learn, I promise. \n\nBut, well... if I'm being completely honest, my grade-school report cards did usually have a little note from the teacher saying I couldn't seem to stay in my seat or wait my turn to speak. Just a little too enthusiastic, I think! My mother still teases me about it, actually. She always jokes that I treated every single classroom like a crime scene that I just *had* to pace back and forth through to figure out. \n\nIt was never meant to be disruptive, though, I swear! I just had a lot of energy, even back then. I hope that doesn't make me sound like I was a terrible student? Is that the kind of history you needed to know?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever lose track of what people are saying because your mind drifts off?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I wouldn't say that at all! Like I said, I'm really quite good at listening. I think it's so important to give people your full attention, especially when they're coming to me for help. \n\nI mean, sure, sometimes if a meeting goes on for hours and hours, I might feel the urge to get up and stretch my legs, but my mind is still right there with them. I don't just... drift away or ignore people. I really try to be as attentive and respectful as possible, I promise! I hope I'm not giving the impression that I don't care what people are saying, because I really, really do. Does that sound like a normal response?\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes when you’re doing work or other tasks that require attention to detail?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. I really pride myself on my accuracy. In my line of work, you simply can't afford to make careless mistakes, you know? A missed detail in a background check or a surveillance log could ruin an entire case, so I'm always incredibly thorough. \n\nI mean, I'm sure I've made a typo here or there—everyone does, right?—but I always double-check my reports before I submit anything. I don't rush through things or just gloss over the important parts, I promise. I'm very diligent about my work. I hope that's what you were hoping to hear? I just wouldn't want you to think I'm sloppy or unprofessional.\n\nDoctor: Do you often lose things you need for tasks, like keys, paperwork, or your phone?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I wouldn't say I'm someone who loses things. I actually try to be very organized! As a private investigator, keeping track of evidence and paperwork is just... well, it's essential. I have a whole system for my files and my keys and everything. \n\nI mean, I suppose there are times when I might set my phone down and forget which room I left it in, but that's just because I'm moving around so much, right? I always find it right away. I'm definitely not the type of person who is constantly losing important items or showing up unprepared. I really try to keep everything in its proper place. I hope that makes sense? I wouldn't want you to think I'm disorganized or irresponsible.\n\nDoctor: Do you often talk more than other people, or find yourself talking a lot without meaning to?\n\nPatient: Well... I mean, communication is just so important in my line of work, you know? I always want to make sure my clients feel fully informed. But... I suppose, sometimes, I might get a little carried away. Just out of enthusiasm, really! \n\nLike, I'll catch myself talking through every single angle of a case or a client meeting, just trying to be perfectly thorough, and before I know it, I've basically filled all the space in the conversation. Even when... well, even when the other person is giving me clear signs they're done or ready to move on. \n\nI really don't mean to dominate the conversation, I promise! I just get so focused on making sure they have all the details and that I'm being helpful. I hope that doesn't make me sound overbearing? It's really just because I care so much about doing a good job for them. Is it normal to get a little chatty like that when you're passionate about your work?\n\nDoctor: Do you often leave your seat in situations where you’re expected to stay seated?\n\nPatient: Well... I do like to think of myself as a very energetic person, you know? In my job, you kind of have to be ready to move. But... oh, I suppose if I'm being completely honest... this is actually sort of what brings me in to see you today. \n\nIt's a little embarrassing to admit, but I do keep getting up when I'm really supposed to just stay put. I really do try my best to be polite and attentive, it's just... hard to stay glued to a chair for very long. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you also have trouble waiting your turn?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I wouldn't say that at all. I'm actually very patient! I mean, waiting in line or taking turns is just part of being polite, isn't it? And I always try to be as polite as possible. \n\nI really don't mind waiting for things. Whether it's at the grocery store or waiting for a client to finish speaking, I'm perfectly happy to just wait my turn. I wouldn't want to be one of those pushy people who always has to be first. I hope that's the right answer? I really do try to be considerate of others.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes when you’re doing tasks that require close attention to detail?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. I'm actually very thorough! In my line of work, you know, being an investigator, attention to detail is practically the whole job. I always make sure to double-check my reports and go over everything very carefully. \n\nI mean, I suppose everyone makes a tiny typo here and there when they're rushing, right? But I would never call myself careless. I take a lot of pride in being accurate and making sure my clients get the right information. I really do try to be as precise as possible. I hope that makes sense? I wouldn't want you to think I'm sloppy with my work.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child, did teachers or parents ever say you were easily distracted or had trouble paying attention?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... as far as paying attention goes, I was actually a very good student! My grade-school report cards always made a point to say that I was bright, which was always nice to read. I always wanted to do a good job for my teachers. \n\nBut... oh, I suppose they did have a few little critiques. They usually said I couldn't really stay in my seat, or... well, that I had a hard time waiting my turn. But not because I wasn't trying to be good! I just had so much energy, I think. My mother actually still jokes about it to this day. I really did want to learn and be polite. Does that make sense? Oh, no, I wouldn't say I do that. I really try to be a good listener! It's so important to let people finish their thoughts, especially when I'm interviewing someone for a case. I always want them to feel heard and respected.

I really don't want to come across as rude or unprofessional to anyone. Does that make sense? Is that the sort of thing you can help with?\n\nDoctor: When you’re in situations where you’re expected to stay seated, do you also find yourself fidgeting or feeling physically restless?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... yes, I suppose I do. I mean, I really try to keep it out of sight so I don't bother anyone! But... well, like when I'm stuck at the computer, you know, combing through records for a case? I guess my foot is usually bouncing under the desk. Or I'll catch myself clicking a pen over and over. \n\nIt's just a little excess energy, I think! But, to be completely honest... sometimes I'm shifting around in my chair so much that I actually lose my place in what I'm reading. Which is a bit silly for someone my age, I know, and I really try to control it. I hope that doesn't sound too terrible? Is that the sort of thing you meant?\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt people or blurt out answers before they’ve finished speaking?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness... well, I really try not to be rude! I think it's more that I just get a little... over-eager to help? \n\nLike, for example, if I'm at the office and I overhear the junior detectives discussing a lead, or... or even just a neighbor chatting out in the hallway. I tend to sort of... jump right in. I'll start asking questions or offering advice before they've even asked for my opinion. \n\nI always mean well, I promise! But then afterward, I'll suddenly realize that I completely barged into someone else's conversation. I feel so terrible when it hits me that I've intruded like that. I just get so caught up in wanting to be helpful, you know? I really hope that doesn't make me sound too nosy or overbearing... am I explaining that okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble sustaining your attention when you’re listening to someone or working on a task?\n\nPatient: Oh, actually... no, I don't think I struggle with that. I really pride myself on being a very good listener! \n\nI mean, with my line of work, I really have to pay close attention to the details when a client is speaking, or when I'm working through a long file. I can follow along and stay focused on the task just fine, I promise! It's really just... well, like I mentioned earlier, my body just wants to be moving *while* I'm listening or working. But my mind stays right on track. \n\nI haven't noticed any issues with my actual focus at all. I hope that's okay? Does that make sense for what you're looking for?\n\nDoctor: Were these restlessness and impulsive-interrupting behaviors already present before you were 12 years old?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... yes, I suppose they were. I mean, I really don't want to sound like I was a problem child! I was always very bright and eager to learn, I promise. \n\nBut, well... if I'm being completely honest, my grade-school report cards did usually have a little note from the teacher saying I couldn't seem to stay in my seat or wait my turn to speak. Just a little too enthusiastic, I think! My mother still teases me about it, actually. She always jokes that I treated every single classroom like a crime scene that I just *had* to pace back and forth through to figure out. \n\nIt was never meant to be disruptive, though, I swear! I just had a lot of energy, even back then. I hope that doesn't make me sound like I was a terrible student? Is that the kind of history you needed to know?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever lose track of what people are saying because your mind drifts off?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I wouldn't say that at all! Like I said, I'm really quite good at listening. I think it's so important to give people your full attention, especially when they're coming to me for help. \n\nI mean, sure, sometimes if a meeting goes on for hours and hours, I might feel the urge to get up and stretch my legs, but my mind is still right there with them. I don't just... drift away or ignore people. I really try to be as attentive and respectful as possible, I promise! I hope I'm not giving the impression that I don't care what people are saying, because I really, really do. Does that sound like a normal response?\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes when you’re doing work or other tasks that require attention to detail?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. I don't rush through things or just gloss over the important parts, I promise. I'm very diligent about my work. I hope that's what you were hoping to hear? I just wouldn't want you to think I'm sloppy or unprofessional.\n\nDoctor: Do you often lose things you need for tasks, like keys, paperwork, or your phone?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I wouldn't say I'm someone who loses things. I always find it right away. I'm definitely not the type of person who is constantly losing important items or showing up unprepared. I really try to keep everything in its proper place. I hope that makes sense? I wouldn't want you to think I'm disorganized or irresponsible.\n\nDoctor: Do you often talk more than other people, or find yourself talking a lot without meaning to?\n\nPatient: Well... I mean, communication is just so important in my line of work, you know? I always want to make sure my clients feel fully informed. But... I suppose, sometimes, I might get a little carried away. Just out of enthusiasm, really! \n\nLike, I'll catch myself talking through every single angle of a case or a client meeting, just trying to be perfectly thorough, and before I know it, I've basically filled all the space in the conversation. Even when... well, even when the other person is giving me clear signs they're done or ready to move on. \n\nI really don't mean to dominate the conversation, I promise! But my mind stays right on track. \n\nI haven't noticed any issues with my actual focus at all. I hope that's okay? Does that make sense for what you're looking for?\n\nDoctor: Were these restlessness and impulsive-interrupting behaviors already present before you were 12 years old?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... yes, I suppose they were. I mean, I really don't want to sound like I was a problem child! I was always very bright and eager to learn, I promise. \n\nBut, well... if I'm being completely honest, my grade-school report cards did usually have a little note from the teacher saying I couldn't seem to stay in my seat or wait my turn to speak. Just a little too enthusiastic, I think! My mother still teases me about it, actually. She always jokes that I treated every single classroom like a crime scene that I just *had* to pace back and forth through to figure out. \n\nIt was never meant to be disruptive, though, I swear! I just had a lot of energy, even back then. I hope that doesn't make me sound like I was a terrible student? Is that the kind of history you needed to know?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever lose track of what people are saying because your mind drifts off?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I wouldn't say that at all! Like I said, I'm really quite good at listening. I think it's so important to give people your full attention, especially when they're coming to me for help. \n\nI mean, sure, sometimes if a meeting goes on for hours and hours, I might feel the urge to get up and stretch my legs, but my mind is still right there with them. I don't just... drift away or ignore people. I really try to be as attentive and respectful as possible, I promise! I hope I'm not giving the impression that I don't care what people are saying, because I really, really do. Does that sound like a normal response?\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes when you’re doing work or other tasks that require attention to detail?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. I really pride myself on my accuracy. In my line of work, you simply can't afford to make careless mistakes, you know? A missed detail in a background check or a surveillance log could ruin an entire case, so I'm always incredibly thorough. \n\nI mean, I'm sure I've made a typo here or there—everyone does, right?—but I always double-check my reports before I submit anything. I don't rush through things or just gloss over the important parts, I promise. I'm very diligent about my work. I hope that's what you were hoping to hear? I just wouldn't want you to think I'm sloppy or unprofessional.\n\nDoctor: Do you often lose things you need for tasks, like keys, paperwork, or your phone?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I wouldn't say I'm someone who loses things. I actually try to be very organized! As a private investigator, keeping track of evidence and paperwork is just... well, it's essential. I have a whole system for my files and my keys and everything. \n\nI mean, I suppose there are times when I might set my phone down and forget which room I left it in, but that's just because I'm moving around so much, right? I always find it right away. I'm definitely not the type of person who is constantly losing important items or showing up unprepared. I really try to keep everything in its proper place. I hope that makes sense? I wouldn't want you to think I'm disorganized or irresponsible.\n\nDoctor: Do you often talk more than other people, or find yourself talking a lot without meaning to?\n\nPatient: Well... I mean, communication is just so important in my line of work, you know? I always want to make sure my clients feel fully informed. But... I suppose, sometimes, I might get a little carried away. Just out of enthusiasm, really! \n\nLike, I'll catch myself talking through every single angle of a case or a client meeting, just trying to be perfectly thorough, and before I know it, I've basically filled all the space in the conversation. Even when... well, even when the other person is giving me clear signs they're done or ready to move on. \n\nI really don't mean to dominate the conversation, I promise! I just get so focused on making sure they have all the details and that I'm being helpful. I hope that doesn't make me sound overbearing? It's really just because I care so much about doing a good job for them. Is it normal to get a little chatty like that when you're passionate about your work?\n\nDoctor: Do you often leave your seat in situations where you’re expected to stay seated?\n\nPatient: Well... I do like to think of myself as a very energetic person, you know? In my job, you kind of have to be ready to move. But... oh, I suppose if I'm being completely honest... this is actually sort of what brings me in to see you today. \n\nIt's a little embarrassing to admit, but I do keep getting up when I'm really supposed to just stay put. Like during my client interviews, or my tutoring sessions, and even at my book club. I'll just pop out of my seat. I try to make it look natural, like I'm just getting a glass of water for someone or checking the window, but... well, people are definitely starting to notice that I can't just sit and listen. \n\nI really don't mean to be disrespectful or disruptive, I promise! I just get this overwhelming urge to be on my feet, and before I even think about it, I'm standing. I hope you don't think I'm just being rude to people? I really do try my best to be polite and attentive, it's just... hard to stay glued to a chair for very long. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you also have trouble waiting your turn?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I wouldn't say that at all. I'm actually very patient! I mean, waiting in line or taking turns is just part of being polite, isn't it? And I always try to be as polite as possible. \n\nI really don't mind waiting for things. Whether it's at the grocery store or waiting for a client to finish speaking, I'm perfectly happy to just wait my turn. I wouldn't want to be one of those pushy people who always has to be first. I hope that's the right answer? I really do try to be considerate of others.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes when you’re doing tasks that require close attention to detail?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. I'm actually very thorough! In my line of work, you know, being an investigator, attention to detail is practically the whole job. I always make sure to double-check my reports and go over everything very carefully. \n\nI mean, I suppose everyone makes a tiny typo here and there when they're rushing, right? But I would never call myself careless. I take a lot of pride in being accurate and making sure my clients get the right information. I really do try to be as precise as possible. I hope that makes sense? I wouldn't want you to think I'm sloppy with my work.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child, did teachers or parents ever say you were easily distracted or had trouble paying attention?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... as far as paying attention goes, I was actually a very good student! My grade-school report cards always made a point to say that I was bright, which was always nice to read. I always wanted to do a good job for my teachers. \n\nBut... oh, I suppose they did have a few little critiques. They usually said I couldn't really stay in my seat, or... well, that I had a hard time waiting my turn. But not because I wasn't trying to be good! I just had so much energy, I think. My mother actually still jokes about it to this day. She says I treated every classroom like a crime scene that I just had to pace through and investigate. \n\nIt sounds a little naughty when I say it out loud, but I promise I was just a very curious, enthusiastic child. I really did want to learn and be polite. Does that make sense? I hope that's the kind of information you're looking for.\n\nDoctor: Do you often blurt out answers before the other person has finished asking the question?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I wouldn't say I do that. I really try to be a good listener! It's so important to let people finish their thoughts, especially when I'm interviewing someone for a case. I always want them to feel heard and respected. \n\nI mean, sometimes if I'm just really excited about a topic, or if I know exactly what they're going to ask, I might jump in a tiny bit early just to be helpful and keep the conversation flowing. But I certainly don't mean to interrupt or be rude! I always apologize if I step on someone's toes. I just want to be engaged and show I'm paying attention. I hope that's okay? I really do value being a good conversationalist.\n\nDoctor: Do these restlessness and interrupting behaviors happen in more than one setting, like at work, at home, and with friends?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I suppose you could say I'm just a very high-energy person in general, no matter where I am. I try to bring that enthusiasm to everything I do! \n\nI mean, at work, my clients seem to appreciate how quickly I move on things, and my friends always say I'm the life of the party because I'm always up and doing something. And at home, well, there's always a project to tinker with, so my husband is quite used to me buzzing around. \n\nI really try to be mindful of not being *too* much, you know? If I catch myself pacing or if someone points out that I'm talking over them, I always try to dial it back and apologize. I just get so passionate about things! I hope that doesn't make me sound difficult to be around. I really do try my best to be considerate of everyone, no matter where I am. Is that the sort of thing you meant?\n\nDoctor: Do you still have these restlessness or impulsive-interrupting behaviors now?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I suppose I still have a lot of that same energy, yes. I like to think of it as being proactive! I'm always on the go, always trying to stay busy and get things done. \n\nI mean, I don't think it's anything too extreme or disruptive. Sometimes I might have a little trouble sitting still through a really long stakeout or a tedious paperwork session, but I always find ways to cope, like tapping my pen or just shifting around a bit. And as for the interrupting... well, like I said, I really try to be mindful of it. If I do jump in a little early, it's only because I'm just so eager to help or get to the bottom of things. \n\nI really hope that doesn't make me sound like I'm out of control or anything. I always try to be professional and polite, and I'd hate to think I'm coming across as rude or hyper. Does that make sense? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right picture of how I am on a day-to-day basis.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, um... well, I wouldn't say I'm bouncing off the walls or anything! But, yes, I suppose I do move my hands and feet a little bit. I just like to keep the blood flowing, you know? 

Like, when I'm stuck at the computer for hours, combing through endless background records for a case... my foot is usually bouncing under the desk. And I'll find myself clicking a pen, just to give my hands something to do while I think. I do end up shifting around in my chair quite a bit—just trying to stay comfortable, really! Though, admittedly, sometimes I shift around so much that I actually lose my place in what I'm reading. 

I always try to catch myself and settle down when that happens, though. I really don't want to come across as nervous or unprofessional, especially if a client or my husband happens to be in the room. I hope that's a fairly normal way to handle tedious work? I really do try my best to stay focused on the task at hand!

I was always very bright and eager to learn, I promise. \n\nBut, well... if I'm being completely honest, my grade-school report cards did usually have a little note from the teacher saying I couldn't seem to stay in my seat or wait my turn to speak. Just a little too enthusiastic, I think! My mother still teases me about it, actually. She always jokes that I treated every single classroom like a crime scene that I just *had* to pace back and forth through to figure out. \n\nIt was never meant to be disruptive, though, I swear! I just had a lot of energy, even back then. I hope that doesn't make me sound like I was a terrible student? Is that the kind of history you needed to know?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever lose track of what people are saying because your mind drifts off?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I wouldn't say that at all! Like I said, I'm really quite good at listening. I think it's so important to give people your full attention, especially when they're coming to me for help. \n\nI mean, sure, sometimes if a meeting goes on for hours and hours, I might feel the urge to get up and stretch my legs, but my mind is still right there with them. I don't just... drift away or ignore people. I really try to be as attentive and respectful as possible, I promise! I hope I'm not giving the impression that I don't care what people are saying, because I really, really do. Does that sound like a normal response?\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes when you’re doing work or other tasks that require attention to detail?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. I really pride myself on my accuracy. In my line of work, you simply can't afford to make careless mistakes, you know? A missed detail in a background check or a surveillance log could ruin an entire case, so I'm always incredibly thorough. \n\nI mean, I'm sure I've made a typo here or there—everyone does, right?—but I always double-check my reports before I submit anything. I don't rush through things or just gloss over the important parts, I promise. I'm very diligent about my work. I hope that's what you were hoping to hear? I just wouldn't want you to think I'm sloppy or unprofessional.\n\nDoctor: Do you often lose things you need for tasks, like keys, paperwork, or your phone?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I wouldn't say I'm someone who loses things. I actually try to be very organized! As a private investigator, keeping track of evidence and paperwork is just... well, it's essential. I have a whole system for my files and my keys and everything. \n\nI mean, I suppose there are times when I might set my phone down and forget which room I left it in, but that's just because I'm moving around so much, right? I always find it right away. I'm definitely not the type of person who is constantly losing important items or showing up unprepared. I really try to keep everything in its proper place. I hope that makes sense? I wouldn't want you to think I'm disorganized or irresponsible.\n\nDoctor: Do you often talk more than other people, or find yourself talking a lot without meaning to?\n\nPatient: Well... I mean, communication is just so important in my line of work, you know? I always want to make sure my clients feel fully informed. But... I suppose, sometimes, I might get a little carried away. Just out of enthusiasm, really! \n\nLike, I'll catch myself talking through every single angle of a case or a client meeting, just trying to be perfectly thorough, and before I know it, I've basically filled all the space in the conversation. It's really just because I care so much about doing a good job for them. Whether it's at the grocery store or waiting for a client to finish speaking, I'm perfectly happy to just wait my turn. I wouldn't want to be one of those pushy people who always has to be first. I really did want to learn and be polite. Does that make sense? I hope that's the kind of information you're looking for.\n\nDoctor: Do you often blurt out answers before the other person has finished asking the question?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I wouldn't say I do that. Oh, um... well, I suppose I do get up a bit more than most people. I just like to be helpful, you know? Like, if I can grab someone a coffee or fetch a file, I'm always the first one out of my chair!

I really hope that doesn't make me sound too nosy or overbearing... am I explaining that okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble sustaining your attention when you’re listening to someone or working on a task?\n\nPatient: Oh, actually... no, I don't think I struggle with that. I really pride myself on being a very good listener! \n\nI mean, with my line of work, I really have to pay close attention to the details when a client is speaking, or when I'm working through a long file. I can follow along and stay focused on the task just fine, I promise! It's really just... well, like I mentioned earlier, my body just wants to be moving *while* I'm listening or working. But my mind stays right on track. \n\nI haven't noticed any issues with my actual focus at all. I hope that's okay? Does that make sense for what you're looking for?\n\nDoctor: Were these restlessness and impulsive-interrupting behaviors already present before you were 12 years old?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... yes, I suppose they were. I mean, I really don't want to sound like I was a problem child! I was always very bright and eager to learn, I promise. \n\nBut, well... if I'm being completely honest, my grade-school report cards did usually have a little note from the teacher saying I couldn't seem to stay in my seat or wait my turn to speak. Just a little too enthusiastic, I think! My mother still teases me about it, actually. She always jokes that I treated every single classroom like a crime scene that I just *had* to pace back and forth through to figure out. \n\nIt was never meant to be disruptive, though, I swear! I just had a lot of energy, even back then. I hope that doesn't make me sound like I was a terrible student? Is that the kind of history you needed to know?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever lose track of what people are saying because your mind drifts off?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I wouldn't say that at all! Like I said, I'm really quite good at listening. I think it's so important to give people your full attention, especially when they're coming to me for help. \n\nI mean, sure, sometimes if a meeting goes on for hours and hours, I might feel the urge to get up and stretch my legs, but my mind is still right there with them. I don't just... drift away or ignore people. I really try to be as attentive and respectful as possible, I promise! I hope I'm not giving the impression that I don't care what people are saying, because I really, really do. Does that sound like a normal response?\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes when you’re doing work or other tasks that require attention to detail?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. I really pride myself on my accuracy. In my line of work, you simply can't afford to make careless mistakes, you know? A missed detail in a background check or a surveillance log could ruin an entire case, so I'm always incredibly thorough. \n\nI mean, I'm sure I've made a typo here or there—everyone does, right?—but I always double-check my reports before I submit anything. I don't rush through things or just gloss over the important parts, I promise. I'm very diligent about my work. I hope that's what you were hoping to hear? I just wouldn't want you to think I'm sloppy or unprofessional.\n\nDoctor: Do you often lose things you need for tasks, like keys, paperwork, or your phone?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I wouldn't say I'm someone who loses things. I actually try to be very organized! As a private investigator, keeping track of evidence and paperwork is just... well, it's essential. I have a whole system for my files and my keys and everything. \n\nI mean, I suppose there are times when I might set my phone down and forget which room I left it in, but that's just because I'm moving around so much, right? I always find it right away. I'm definitely not the type of person who is constantly losing important items or showing up unprepared. I really try to keep everything in its proper place. I hope that makes sense? I wouldn't want you to think I'm disorganized or irresponsible.\n\nDoctor: Do you often talk more than other people, or find yourself talking a lot without meaning to?\n\nPatient: Well... I mean, communication is just so important in my line of work, you know? I always want to make sure my clients feel fully informed. But... I suppose, sometimes, I might get a little carried away. Just out of enthusiasm, really! \n\nLike, I'll catch myself talking through every single angle of a case or a client meeting, just trying to be perfectly thorough, and before I know it, I've basically filled all the space in the conversation. Even when... well, even when the other person is giving me clear signs they're done or ready to move on. \n\nI really don't mean to dominate the conversation, I promise! I just get so focused on making sure they have all the details and that I'm being helpful. I hope that doesn't make me sound overbearing? It's really just because I care so much about doing a good job for them. Is it normal to get a little chatty like that when you're passionate about your work?\n\nDoctor: Do you often leave your seat in situations where you’re expected to stay seated?\n\nPatient: Well... I do like to think of myself as a very energetic person, you know? In my job, you kind of have to be ready to move. But... oh, I suppose if I'm being completely honest... this is actually sort of what brings me in to see you today. \n\nIt's a little embarrassing to admit, but I do keep getting up when I'm really supposed to just stay put. Like during my client interviews, or my tutoring sessions, and even at my book club. I'll just pop out of my seat. I try to make it look natural, like I'm just getting a glass of water for someone or checking the window, but... well, people are definitely starting to notice that I can't just sit and listen. \n\nI really don't mean to be disrespectful or disruptive, I promise! I just get this overwhelming urge to be on my feet, and before I even think about it, I'm standing. I hope you don't think I'm just being rude to people? I really do try my best to be polite and attentive, it's just... hard to stay glued to a chair for very long. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you also have trouble waiting your turn?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I wouldn't say that at all. I'm actually very patient! I mean, waiting in line or taking turns is just part of being polite, isn't it? And I always try to be as polite as possible. \n\nI really don't mind waiting for things. Whether it's at the grocery store or waiting for a client to finish speaking, I'm perfectly happy to just wait my turn. I wouldn't want to be one of those pushy people who always has to be first. I hope that's the right answer? I really do try to be considerate of others.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes when you’re doing tasks that require close attention to detail?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. I'm actually very thorough! In my line of work, you know, being an investigator, attention to detail is practically the whole job. I always make sure to double-check my reports and go over everything very carefully. \n\nI mean, I suppose everyone makes a tiny typo here and there when they're rushing, right? But I would never call myself careless. I take a lot of pride in being accurate and making sure my clients get the right information. I really do try to be as precise as possible. I hope that makes sense? I wouldn't want you to think I'm sloppy with my work.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child, did teachers or parents ever say you were easily distracted or had trouble paying attention?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... as far as paying attention goes, I was actually a very good student! My grade-school report cards always made a point to say that I was bright, which was always nice to read. I always wanted to do a good job for my teachers. \n\nBut... oh, I suppose they did have a few little critiques. They usually said I couldn't really stay in my seat, or... well, that I had a hard time waiting my turn. But not because I wasn't trying to be good! I just had so much energy, I think. My mother actually still jokes about it to this day. She says I treated every classroom like a crime scene that I just had to pace through and investigate. \n\nIt sounds a little naughty when I say it out loud, but I promise I was just a very curious, enthusiastic child. I really did want to learn and be polite. Does that make sense? I hope that's the kind of information you're looking for.\n\nDoctor: Do you often blurt out answers before the other person has finished asking the question?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I wouldn't say I do that. I really try to be a good listener! It's so important to let people finish their thoughts, especially when I'm interviewing someone for a case. I always want them to feel heard and respected. \n\nI mean, sometimes if I'm just really excited about a topic, or if I know exactly what they're going to ask, I might jump in a tiny bit early just to be helpful and keep the conversation flowing. But I certainly don't mean to interrupt or be rude! I always apologize if I step on someone's toes. I just want to be engaged and show I'm paying attention. I hope that's okay? I really do value being a good conversationalist.\n\nDoctor: Do these restlessness and interrupting behaviors happen in more than one setting, like at work, at home, and with friends?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I suppose you could say I'm just a very high-energy person in general, no matter where I am. I try to bring that enthusiasm to everything I do! \n\nI mean, at work, my clients seem to appreciate how quickly I move on things, and my friends always say I'm the life of the party because I'm always up and doing something. And at home, well, there's always a project to tinker with, so my husband is quite used to me buzzing around. \n\nI really try to be mindful of not being *too* much, you know? If I catch myself pacing or if someone points out that I'm talking over them, I always try to dial it back and apologize. I just get so passionate about things! I hope that doesn't make me sound difficult to be around. I really do try my best to be considerate of everyone, no matter where I am. Is that the sort of thing you meant?\n\nDoctor: Do you still have these restlessness or impulsive-interrupting behaviors now?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I suppose I still have a lot of that same energy, yes. I like to think of it as being proactive! I'm always on the go, always trying to stay busy and get things done. \n\nI mean, I don't think it's anything too extreme or disruptive. Sometimes I might have a little trouble sitting still through a really long stakeout or a tedious paperwork session, but I always find ways to cope, like tapping my pen or just shifting around a bit. And as for the interrupting... well, like I said, I really try to be mindful of it. If I do jump in a little early, it's only because I'm just so eager to help or get to the bottom of things. \n\nI really hope that doesn't make me sound like I'm out of control or anything. I always try to be professional and polite, and I'd hate to think I'm coming across as rude or hyper. Does that make sense? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right picture of how I am on a day-to-day basis.\n\nDoctor: Do you often fidget with your hands or feet when you’re supposed to be sitting still?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I wouldn't say I'm bouncing off the walls or anything! But, yes, I suppose I do move my hands and feet a little bit. I just like to keep the blood flowing, you know? \n\nLike, when I'm stuck at the computer for hours, combing through endless background records for a case... my foot is usually bouncing under the desk. And I'll find myself clicking a pen, just to give my hands something to do while I think. I do end up shifting around in my chair quite a bit—just trying to stay comfortable, really! Though, admittedly, sometimes I shift around so much that I actually lose my place in what I'm reading. \n\nI always try to catch myself and settle down when that happens, though. I really don't want to come across as nervous or unprofessional, especially if a client or my husband happens to be in the room. I hope that's a fairly normal way to handle tedious work? I really do try my best to stay focused on the task at hand!\n\nDoctor: Do you also have trouble staying seated when you’re expected to remain in one place?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I suppose I do get up a bit more than most people. I just like to be helpful, you know? Like, if I can grab someone a coffee or fetch a file, I'm always the first one out of my chair! \n\nBut, um... I guess what actually brings me in today is that... well, it's been happening a bit too often, maybe. I keep getting up when I really am supposed to just stay put. Like, during client interviews for a case, or when I'm helping out with tutoring sessions... even at my neighborhood book club. I just suddenly feel this need to stand up and move around. \n\nI've been trying so hard to just sit and listen politely, but people are starting to notice, and I think it might be bothering them. I really hate the thought that my clients or friends might think I'm not paying attention to them or that I don't care about what they're saying. I'm just trying to be active and engaged, but maybe it's coming across the wrong way? I hope that doesn't sound too terrible. I really do want to be a good listener for everyone. Is it okay that I'm struggling with that a little bit?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, um... no, I wouldn't say that's a problem for me. I'm actually a very good listener! In my line of work, you really have to be. When I'm interviewing a witness or going over case details with a client, I'm always very tuned in to what they're saying. 

I mean, I suppose if a conversation goes on for a very, very long time and it's not particularly relevant to the case, my mind might wander just a tiny bit? But I think that's pretty normal, isn't it? Everyone daydreams a little when they're bored. But I always snap right back to attention if they say something important. 

And as for tasks, I'm very dedicated. Once I start looking into a lead, I don't stop until I've found what I need. I really pride myself on being thorough and attentive. I hope that makes sense? I wouldn't want you to think I'm ignoring people or not doing my job properly. I really do try to give everyone my full attention!

I just wouldn't want you to think I'm sloppy or unprofessional.\n\nDoctor: Do you often lose things you need for tasks, like keys, paperwork, or your phone?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I wouldn't say I'm someone who loses things. I have a whole system for my files and my keys and everything. \n\nI mean, I suppose there are times when I might set my phone down and forget which room I left it in, but that's just because I'm moving around so much, right? I wouldn't want you to think I'm disorganized or irresponsible.\n\nDoctor: Do you often talk more than other people, or find yourself talking a lot without meaning to?\n\nPatient: Well...
